WESTPAC ECONOMICS
THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: whiplash
total to $11.1bn. This is another clear marker of the scale of
Australia’s data-centre investment boom, which should feature
prominently in both business investment and imports in the Q3
National Accounts.
Offshore, the world has been focused on the US mid-term

elections. In the House, Senate and a series of State Governor
races, the Democrats experienced resounding victory. Come
2027, the Democrats will have a comfortable majority in both
houses of Congress and are expected to make use of this
strength to respond to voter concerns, particularly over cost-
of-living pressures. The election result also limits President
Trump’s ability to fund an extended military action against
Iran. Notably, both immediately before and after the election,

President Trump and the administration have exhibited a greater
interest in furthering negotiations with Iran. It is therefore not
a surprise that Brent oil has backed off its most recent highs
around USD118 last Friday, although the scale of the drop to
near USD93 yesterday is arguably too optimistic. Brent has since
traded back up to above USD99, still 16% below Friday’s peak.
While the initial reaction to the US election result was a
robust broad-based rally in major equity indexes, concerns

over the potential for tighter regulation of AI and the related
infrastructure buildout jolted US equities lower overnight – the
Nasdaq falling 5.5% and S&P500 4.4%, both a multiple of
the prior session’s gains. That said, US term interest rates and
FOMC rate expectations have also been scaled back; so, while
tech may come under greater scrutiny, a foundation for better
outcomes across the rest of the US economy may become
evident in time. US data out this week continued to suggest

their economy is in robust health overall. And, if tensions in
the Middle East abate, the FOMC’s concerns over US inflation
should subside. Gaining enough comfort for the FOMC to not
hike in December is a high bar, but the probability of further
hikes in 2027 should continue to fall.
Turning then to the UK, the Bank of England raised Bank Rate
by 25bps to 4.00% at their November meeting as expected.
On the Bank's baseline expectation, UK inflation is forecast to

rise from 3.5%yr at September to 5.00% by mid-2027. Activity
data has also surprised to the upside in recent months, though
"there remains limited evidence of knock-on effects on other
prices and wages".
In Australia, the RBA delivered another 25bp rate increase
at its November meeting, taking the cash rate to 4.85%. The
move was widely expected, reflecting the Board’s continued
concern around inflation following the recent run higher in
global energy prices, firmer domestic capacity pressures and

signs that inflation expectations have edged up. Inflation
is now not expected to return to the mid-point of the
target band until mid-2028. This is despite weaker growth
expectations and the unemployment rate trending up to
5.1% by end-2028 on the RBA’s forecasts.
Importantly, though, the decision was not unanimous, with
three members preferring to remain on hold given downside
risks to activity and the restraining influence of slower growth.
Governor Bullock’s suggestion that it was time to “take stock”

after five rate increases also struck a less hawkish note,
while the statement dropped September’s explicit reference
to raising rates further “if needed”. In our view, February
represents the next plausible opportunity for another hike, but
we see this only as a risk.
Policy’s effect on housing is becoming increasingly visible.
Cotality home prices fell a further 1.2% in October to now
be 6.7% below March’s peak. The cumulative price decline
is approaching the scale of previous housing corrections but

appears to have regained momentum after September’s rate
increase. Dwelling approvals saw a 5.1% headline gain in
September, but this was driven by volatile unit approvals while
detached house approvals fell sharply, suggesting higher rates
and the established-market downturn are beginning to weigh
on the construction pipeline.
Official data on nominal household spending meanwhile
rose 0.6% in September and 2.1% for Q3; however, much of
the recent acceleration looks to be price-driven, particularly

for fuel. Real spending volumes expanded by a much more
modest (but still solid) 0.7% in Q3.
The Westpac-DataX Consumer Panel provides a granular look at
income, spending and saving flows. The latest update suggests
that, beneath the resilient headline story, households are
increasingly being squeezed, particularly those with low savings
buffers. With another rate increase now delivered, housing
wealth declining and sentiment weak, Q3 increasingly looks like

the high-water mark for growth in consumer spending.
Lastly for Australia, in September the goods trade surplus
widened sharply to $2.8bn, with non-monetary gold trade
yet again adding considerable noise month-to-month. The
most notable development was another exceptionally strong
result for data-processing equipment imports, taking the Q3
Elliot Clarke, Head of International Economics

In the press conference, Governor Bailey recognised the
nascent improvement in energy price pressures over the
past 48 hours and the effectiveness of monetary policy but
also conveyed that "Unless progress towards resolving the
conflict is made, or there is other news which softens the risk

to inflationary pressures, a further increase in Bank Rate may
be warranted". Governor Bailey's tone and comments made in
the minutes by other Committee members did not suggest this
was a decision that needs to be made in December, but rather
a risk to be judged as additional information is received. This
points to further tightening being more likely in early 2027
than before, and only if conditions / risks warrant.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
No deals are done
This week’s developments underscore our message that
a rate hike at the RBNZ’s December meeting is far from a
done deal. New Zealand’s labour market remains softer
than expected, and that will be noted by those on the

policy committee who question the extent of second-round
inflation pressures. The rout in the US mid-term elections
has also taken significant heat out of oil prices for now,
although it’s unclear whether that situation will hold through
to next month. Our view on the endpoint for this OCR cycle
remains unchanged, but there’s a growing risk that the RBNZ
could slow the pace of tightening in the new year.
The September quarter labour market surveys pointed to

ongoing softness in the jobs market. The unemployment
rate rose from 5.6% to 5.7%, continuing a four-year run
of increases, and reaching its highest level since 2015.
Employment was softer than expected, and wage growth
remains subdued despite increasing cost-of-living pressures.
The Household Labour Force Survey (HLFS) showed a surprise
0.1% fall in employment in the September quarter, after a
downwardly revised 0.3% increase in the June quarter. That

went against the Monthly Employment Indicator (MEI) measure
of filled jobs, which had suggested some upward momentum
emerging in the jobs market (and notably, the HLFS measure
had outpaced the MEI for the previous nine straight quarters).
The fall in HLFS employment was accompanied by a fall in
the labour force participation rate from 70.6% to 70.4%.
These co-movements essentially reversed the surprisingly
large increases that we saw in the June quarter, which we had

suggested at the time were more likely due to sampling error
in the survey rather than a genuine surge.
We generally recommend focusing on the unemployment rate,
which is less vulnerable to sampling errors – and the message
here is one of softness. Our best interpretation is that, to the
extent that jobs are growing, it’s not fast enough to absorb the
growth in the population (which itself is a fairly low bar, as net
migration remains at a low ebb).

Even with the economy returning to growth over the last
year or so, hiring activity has remained weak. This may be
an ongoing legacy of employment trends in previous years.
Businesses have told us that they felt burned by the severe
skill shortages that developed over 2021-23, and looked to
hold on to workers to the extent they could during the 2024-
25 downturn. The flipside of this is that they may now find
themselves overstaffed even as activity picks up again.

This shows up most strikingly in labour force transition rates.
The rise in unemployment over the last few years has not
been driven by an increase in job losses, but by accumulation
of people who, once out of work, are finding it increasingly
difficult to get back in again. That also extends to young
people trying to enter the workforce for the first time – youth
unemployment of around 25% is the highest in the history of
the survey going back to 1986.

The Labour Cost Index (LCI) rose by 0.5% in the September
quarter, in line with our expectations overall but a bit weaker
in the details – private sector wages were up by 0.5%, while
there was a surprisingly large 0.7% increase in public sector
wages. There was a notable jump in the number of employers
citing cost-of-living adjustments as a reason for increasing pay
rates – although the size of those increases varied a great deal,
with many substantially less than the headline inflation rate.

Potential RBNZ implications
The labour market data was unequivocally weaker than
expected by either us or the RBNZ. While likely not a
gamechanger for the cycle overall, it will add ammunition to the
arguments of the doves on the Monetary Policy Committee that
a pause in interest rate rises will make sense soon – perhaps in
December or early next year. It’s likely the case that interest rate
markets were over-pricing the odds of a hike at the 9 December

MPS, so the subsequent pullback in pricing makes sense.
The message from the RBNZ in the October meeting was to
be careful about pricing in "done deals", and that the outlook
Michael Gordon
Senior Economist

### Page 7

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
7
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
will be data-dependent. As we noted at the time, alongside
measures of inflation expectations, the key domestic data
to watch ahead of the RBNZ’s 9 December meeting will
be indicators of what is happening to excess capacity (the
output gap), as this will be a key determinant of the Bank’s

assessment of the outlook for inflation over the coming year.
The RBNZ looks at a broad suite of indicators of labour market
slack, although their research found that only four of them
seem to have predictive power: the unemployment rate,
the vacancy to unemployment ratio, the QSBO measure of
labour as the main limiting factor to growth, and the rate of
transition between jobs. The first three of these all pointed
to a softening in the September quarter. (The fourth one is

calculated from income tax data, so it’s not publicly available
and even the RBNZ receives it only with a lag.)
In the eyes of the dovish MPC members, the softness in
the labour market indicators will likely push in the direction
of having greater comfort with the medium-term inflation
outlook. The outcome of the election, with the possibility of a
return to a dual inflation/employment mandate, could increase
the prominence of these factors. This likely won’t be an explicit

factor in the RBNZ’s reasoning at its December review, but it
may be in the minds of individual MPC members.
That said, it’s important to remember that in the RBNZ’s
forecasting framework, the GDP figures are the main
determinant of excess capacity in the economy. The most
recent GDP data tells us that the output gap probably
narrowed a bit through the June quarter, mainly reflecting
historical revisions. In light of the latest labour market figures,

there may be some downside risk to the outlook for Q3 GDP –
but there are conflicting indicators there, so it’s not that clear.
At last month’s surprise on-hold OCR decision, Governor
Breman lamented that there was no protocol for the RBNZ
to be able to signal how it was reading the economic data –
in particular the softer than expected Q3 CPI print – ahead
of the policy meeting. In that light, it will be interesting to
see whether they use some already-scheduled speaking

engagements to signal what they make of the labour market
data. Top of mind is Chief Economist Conway's 10 November
speech to the UBS Australasia Conference (pointedly titled
“How is the NZ economy tracking”). Governor Breman is also
speaking to the Institute of Directors on 19 November.
Our view remains that the December OCR hike is more likely
than not. But there will be a clear debate to be had between
the MPC hawks and doves, and it may again come down to a

vote. We still expect a 4% peak in the OCR for this cycle, but
the time taken to get there could be more flexible. Takes along
the lines of "3% is the terminal rate" seem rather hasty on the
basis of a single data release.
Aside from how the Committee interprets the labour market
data, the key considerations in December will be: the RBNZ’s
surveys of inflation expectations by households and businesses;
whether the US mid-term elections lead to a sustained de-

escalation in the Middle East and a pullback in oil prices; and
whether developments in the AI space exacerbate some MPC
members’ concerns about the global economic environment.
And then of course there’s the result of the General Election,
with voting closing on Saturday evening. The actual formation
of a government is likely to take weeks of negotiation, so the
outcome may not be known by the 9 December MPS but at least
the range of possibilities should be clearer. We’ll publish our

initial thoughts on the election results early on Monday morning.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
NZ: Oct Retail Card Spending (%mth)
Nov 12, Last: –0.4, Westpac f/c: 1.4
We’re forecasting a 1.4% rise in retail spending in October.
While that would be a solid increase, it’s in large part due to
the 11% rise in petrol prices over the past month. Stripping out

fuel spending, we’re forecasting a 0.6% rise in core spending
categories. Core spending has been very choppy recently,
and the increase we’re assuming follows falls in the past few
months. A result in line with our expectations would leave the
trend in core spending relatively flat since the start of this year
with the rise in fuel prices weighing on spending in other areas.
AUS: Nov Westpac-MI Consumer Sentiment (index)
Nov 10, Last: 84.4
Consumer sentiment fell 4.7% to 80.4 in Oct, returning to the

deeply pessimistic levels seen earlier in the year. Responses
over the course of the survey week showed a very sharp
deterioration after the RBA rate hike.
The Nov survey is in the field over the week ended Nov 7.
Interest rates will again factor with the RBA raising the cash
rate by another 25bps to 4.85%, an 18yr high. Average pump
prices also pushed higher, the national average just under
$2.40/litre, while the housing market downturn has continued

to broaden and deepen. The US mid-term election results may
have some effects on sentiment locally as well.
AUS: Q3 Housing Finance Approvals (%qtr)
Nov 11 Last: –5.2, Westpac f/c: –10
Market f/c: –8.5, Range: –15 to –5
The total value of housing finance approvals fell The value of
new dwelling finance approvals excl. refinance fell 5.2%qtr,
the largest quarterly drop in three years. Annual growth eased
to 7.3%yr. The weakness was accounted for mainly by lower

lending volumes: loan numbers were down 4.0%qtr (–0.6%yr),
while average loan size also contributed negatively.
Q3 is expected to see an even bigger decline with the full
impact of interest rate rises and tax policy changes showing
through. The value of both loan enquiries and turnover fell by
around 10% in the quarter. We expect total finance approvals
to show a 10% drop as well, with investor loan approvals likely
to be down 16%qtr and owner occupier approvals down 6%qtr.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
US: Oct CPI (%mth)
Nov 11, Headline, Last: 0.6, Market f/c: 0.3, Westpac f/c: 0.4
US consumer prices rose by 0.6% in September, although
half of the gain was due to energy inflation, with core prices
instead up 0.3%. Over the year, prices were reported to have

risen 3.7%yr overall and 2.6%yr excluding food and energy.
Constructively, ex food, energy and shelter, annual inflation
met the FOMC’s medium-term target of 2.0%yr.
Come October, energy price pressures are set to recede
given the pre-election run higher in oil prices came late in the
month. A softer print for shelter is also likely to moderate core
momentum. We see a 0.4% gain for the headline measure and
a 0.2% result for core inflation as most probable. Looking to

2027, disinflation is expected to continue at a measured pace
towards the medium-term target.

## Document 343: FX Daily_ Carry trade unwind in full swing

Source PDF: all report pdf/FX Daily_ Carry trade unwind in full swing.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-11-20
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The prospect of faster Fed tightening and continued dislocation
in tech stocks is sucking funds into the dollar and the yen.
Popular FX carry trade positions are being unwound across the
board.  Given that a hawkish Fed lies at the heart of the
current correction in risk assets, and there are no signs of
Fed rhetoric softening anytime soon, USD can stay strong

Chris Turner, Francesco Pesole and Frantisek Taborsky
The prospect of faster Fed tightening is starting to weigh heavily on risk assets
and helping the dollar
USD: The Waller correction
After another day of losses, the US S&P 500 index is now 8.5% oﬀ
its highs of the year. The extent of this correction is not far from that
seen in March this year (9.5%) and way oﬀ the 'Liberation Day' rout
of 21% seen in March/April 2025. The cause of the current correction
has been the threat of a faster Federal Reserve tightening cycle, put
on the table by the Fed's Christopher Waller last week. We
discussed high US real interest rates here yesterday. And the
volatility in tech stocks may also be owing to this week's Anthropic

### Page 2

IPO, where investors have probably been shuﬄing portfolios to
participate in a $100bn IPO. The fact that the Fed wants to tighten
faster because of a demand shock should not necessarily be bad
news for equities and, barring some major earning misses, the case
for another 10% correction in equities is a weak one.
Yet there seems no reason for the Fed to drop its hawkish rhetoric
anytime soon. Next Wednesday sees the release of the Fed's
preferred inﬂation measure – the core PCE index. The October data

is expected to show the year-on-year rate rising to 3.2% with
upside risks, and conﬁrm that the Fed will need to take action.
For FX, that means a continued strong dollar and continued
pressure on popular carry trades. The Latam complex has had a
terrible week, with the most popular carry position, the Colombian
peso, oﬀ heavily. Brazil yesterday stepped in with FX intervention,
but $1bn in sales was quickly overwhelmed. Elsewhere, the big drop
in metals has left the South African rand vulnerable, and a dovish

central bank has seen an unwind of crowded positions in Hungary's
forint. We are also starting to see expectations build for rate cuts in
Australia next year on the view that the Reserve Bank of Australia
has overtightened. We highlighted this as one of the key risks in our
2027 FX Outlook. Pressure may also be building on long positions in
the Turkish lira, where implied yields are starting to rise again.
A big carry trade unwind and emerging markets sell-oﬀ remain a

positive for the dollar, and to a lesser degree, the yen. We expect the
dollar to hold gains into next week and keep 104.60 as a near-term
DXY target.
The US data calendar is light today, and the only Fed speakers are
the hawkish Waller (10:15CET) and Lisa Cook, who surprisingly
voted for a hike at the October FOMC meeting.
Chris Turner

Chris Turner
GBP: New EU-UK agreements announced
At a press conference in Brussels today, UK Prime Minister Andy
Burnham and EU leaders are expected to announce three new
agreements. The ﬁrst is on SPS (Sanitary and Phytosanitary
measures), which will reduce border checks on trade in food,

animals and plants. The second is on a greater alignment between
the separate EU and UK Emissions Trading Schemes (ETS), where
greater recognition of each other's schemes can reduce carbon-
related friction in trade – especially important for the steel and
manufacturing sectors. The third looks at the thorny issue of
mobility in the Youth Experience Scheme (YES), where up to
100,000 18-31 year-olds can obtain work visas for up to three
years. Burnham will be marketing these deals as addressing the

cost-of-living crisis and giving young Britons valuable opportunities
overseas.

## Document 348: Global Equity Strategy_ 2027 Outlook_ Finding Value in Growth

Source PDF: all report pdf/Global Equity Strategy_ 2027 Outlook_ Finding Value in Growth.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs Publishing
Date: 2026-11-12
Pages: 20
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

2026 was the year of strong earnings. Global equity returns were unusually n
broad-based across regions, supported primarily by robust earnings growth — 
valuations have moderated. 
Earnings growth is poised to slow. As we enter 2027, earnings growth is on n
course to slow sharply across all major regions, growth in AI capex spending is 
likely to peak and the cost of capital remains elevated ... 
... But equities have de-rated sharply; with more attractive valuations, n
positive but slower nominal economic and earnings growth, and lower bond 
yields, inflation and oil prices, we expect equities to continue to rise, but 
with high volatility. 
We focus on Growth balanced with selective Defensives and beneficiaries of n
lower bond yields. 
Attractive opportunities are emerging in long-duration Growth companies, n
which should become more valuable as growth rates slow, particularly US 
Hyperscalers, which continue to enjoy strong (although slower) earnings growth 
and balance sheets. 
We balance this with selective defensive areas that offer reasonable valuations n
and areas that benefit from potential lower bond yields and oil prices. 
While forecast returns are highest in Asia, we focus on risk-adjusted returns and n
are overweight the US and Japan, neutral in Asia and EM, and underweight 
Europe, reflecting relative growth, profitability and valuation opportunities. 
Risks are becoming more two-sided. Bonds, oil prices and AI capex shifts could n
all increase volatility. We recommend diversification across Growth and 
selective Defensive strategies.

### Page 2

Value in Defensive Growth as earnings slow

Our theme for 2026 focused on a broadening equity bull market, with greater 
participation across regions, sectors and themes. Since then, the equity bull market has 
continued, and has widened out across the major regions despite rising oil prices and 
bond yields. The main driver has been strong corporate earnings growth across all 
regions. As we look to 2027, profit and AI capex growth look poised to slow in most 
regions. Nevertheless, recent price action has reflected this slowdown and equity 
valuations have come down substantially, particularly in the Technology sector. 
With economic activity and earnings on course to grow in 2027, albeit at a slower pace 
in nominal terms, and given the prospect of moderating inflation, bond yields and oil 
prices, we expect the equity bull market to continue. The range of potential outcomes 
has widened and, given stock concentration in the US and Asia, is particularly 
dependent on AI capex progress. Our recommended weightings reflect our 
assessment of risk-adjusted returns. We focus on: 
Growth — as earnings growth rates slow and margins moderate, investors are n
likely to reward these companies more highly, particularly as valuations become 
more attractive. AI-related companies have de-rated sharply, reflecting anxiety 
about peak AI capex spend and the possibility of slower returns. This is leaving 
attractive entry points, particularly in large capex Hyperscalers, which have strong 
balance sheets and diversified income streams. Significant upside potential has also 
opened up in more cyclical growth areas in memory and chips, although this comes 
with much higher risks and likely volatility.  
On a risk-adjusted basis, we are overweight the US and Japan, neutral Asia and n
EM, and underweight Europe.  
2026 — the year of earnings 
Key developments in 2026 form the foundations for a shift in opportunities in 2027. 
We see three standout features of global equity markets in 2026. 
Diversification has worked. Equity returns have been reasonable and very 1.
similar across all regions — reversing the trend between 2009 and 2025 when 
equity returns were consistently dominated by US outperformance. 
Strong earnings growth has been the principal driver of equity returns. 2.
All equity markets have de-rated, experiencing lower valuations. 3.
1. Diversified returns 
Overall, the past 12 months have seen broad-based returns across the regions. As 
Exhibit 1 shows, most equity markets have generated very similar total returns (the 
orange diamonds). This reverses the trend that had dominated post financial crisis 
through to 2025, when the US consistently outperformed other regions, benefiting from 
a combination of strong earnings growth and re-rating (as most of the businesses at the 
time were capital-light and high ROE). 
12 November 2026   2
Goldman Sachs Global Equity Strategy

### Page 3

2. Earnings have been the main driver of returns 
Earnings revisions have also been strong across the board (Exhibit 2). There are broadly 
three key drivers of this strength. First, Technology earnings have continued to grow 
robustly; second, higher oil prices have boosted earnings across the commodity 
sector; and third, the spill-over effects of the Technology capex boom have 
generally boosted earnings in HALO (heavy asset, low obsolescence) industries, 
such as Industrials. These factors have also contributed to an unusual pattern of 
earnings upgrades for 2026, 2027. So far, despite the recent equity sell-off, earnings 
revisions have also increased for 2028.

While this has been true in every region, the strongest upgrades have come in EM, largely 
reflecting huge upgrades in the Semiconductor space. This matters, because outsized 
gains in Tech Hardware stocks have pushed EM index concentration to record highs — 
Korea and Taiwan now account for about half of the MSCI EM, with Info Tech at >40% 
and the top 3 stocks (the ‘AI trio’ of TSMC, Samsung Electronics and SK Hynix) at ~30%. 
The recent sell-off in chip names, reflecting fears of a slower roll-out of data centres, has 
pushed valuations down sharply, creating good upside convexity — although the range 
of outcomes is wide and highly dependent on the pace of AI capex growth. See from our 
Equity analysts: At the center of tensions around the AI datacenter build: Searching for value 
following the sector-wide pullback. 
3. Equity markets have de-rated 
Equity valuations have fallen across the board. While the US equity market remains the 
most expensive, it has moderated from close to its 100th percentile over 20 years to a 
P/E of 18.5x.  Asian markets and EM are now trading at P/E ratios well below their 
historical averages. Overall, P/E ratios are around their average over the past 35 years, 
and a similar pattern is true of PEG ratios (adjusting valuations for expected growth) 
(Exhibit 3). Of course, much of the fall in valuations, particularly in the 
Tech-concentrated US and North Asia equity markets, has been the principal driver of 
this de-rating.

The key drivers for 2027 
Equity markets typically rise so long as nominal GDP is growing, valuations are not 
excessive, and interest rates are not rising sharply.  Nevertheless, the momentum of 
growth matters. The shift lower in equity markets in recent weeks — centred around AI 
concerns — has rapidly priced in the prospect of weaker growth rates, and the sharp 
sector and factor rotations within equities have pushed up the valuation of Defensive 
and Value parts of the market. From here, we expect equities to generate positive 
returns (Exhibit 4), albeit with higher volatility than in recent years. We expect investors 
to seek companies that can sustain growth as it becomes more scarce.

Theme 1: Earnings growth is on course to slow 
Our economists expect a slowing in nominal GDP growth rates in 2027 as the AI 
investment boom peaks. At the same time, nearly all markets are likely to see their 
earnings growth rates halve at least (Exhibit 5): in the US, slowing from 36% to 11%, 
Europe from just around 18% to 7%, Japan from 24% to 9% and Asia from close to

Why should earnings growth slow down? 
(a) Hyperscaler capex growth should slow, which should result in decelerating 
earnings growth (Exhibit 6) for much of the AI infrastructure complex and weaken the 
support for some of the ‘picks and shovels’ of the AI-related capex boom, including 
Industrials. Consensus estimates show a slowdown in IT earnings growth from close to 
90% in 2026 to 45% in 2027 and expect Communication Services to slow from growth of 
34% in 2026 to -5% in 2027. (b) Furthermore, depreciation charges should rise 
alongside other costs, such as interest charges. Together, headwinds to growth should 
boost the attractiveness of companies that can sustain higher growth and stable 
margins. This would be relatively good for the US Hyperscalers (whose valuations have 
fallen), but raises more risks for the chip names and Industrial Cyclicals, which have 
enjoyed the benefit of accelerating AI-related capex.

Theme 2: Cost of capital is higher 
Why is the cost of capital higher? 
First, higher inflation has pushed up policy rates. Our rates strategists expect to see a 1.
modest tightening cycle, but slow progress towards cutting interest rates in 2027. 
Second, we have argued that this has reflected the combined impact of a surge in 2.
private sector demand for capital and rising government borrowing. Our bond 
strategists do see some relief in longer-term rates, which should be more supportive 
for equities (Exhibit 8) but our credit strategists expect credit spreads to widen from 
current levels.

Investment Themes 
Broadly, we would focus on three areas within equities: Growth, selective Defensives and 
beneficiaries of lower oil prices and bond yields. The expressions that best fit these 
themes vary somewhat across regions and there is some overlap, particularly between 
defensive areas and bond beneficiaries. Exhibit 9 shows a high level summary. More 
details can be found in the specific regional outlook reports.

### Page 9

Growth 
1) Growth versus Value 
For most of the period after the financial crisis through to the end of Covid-19, equity 
markets were dominated by Growth companies. A combination of surging technology 
profitability and ultra-low interest rates favoured Growth. At the same time, 
disinflationary pressures coupled with low energy prices and poor Banks returns 
(particularly in Europe), became headwinds for Value. Since 2022, however, the 
patterns have changed. Growth has still done well, but rising inflation and interest rates, 
coupled with higher energy prices and a capex boom, have strengthened the 
performance of many Value sectors. In all regions Value has sharply outperformed 
Growth in recent months (Exhibit 10).

Investment focus: Selective Defensives  
The economic outlook remains resilient, but risks are skewed to the downside. Slower 
EPS growth, tighter financial conditions, elevated borrowing costs and the risk of an 
earlier turn in the AI cycle argue for greater portfolio resilience. Bond yields should 
decline modestly, but credit spreads could widen as growth slows. However, Cyclicals 
have already underperformed, while many traditional Defensives no longer look cheap

(Exhibit 16).

We favour selective Defensives rather than a broad defensive shift.  We focus on 
defensive sectors that would also benefit from lower bond yields. Utilities and Telecoms 
in Europe, Healthcare and companies consistently buying back stock in the US, and Asian 
power companies are examples. Quality should benefit from slower growth and tighter 
financial conditions, but valuation discipline remains important. High dispersion should 
create a better environment for stock-picking, with a focus on stable earnings, structural 
growth, strong balance sheets and shareholder returns. Areas of focus include: US—
Healthcare, Power and Buyback Aristocrats; Asia—Shareholder-return strategies, 
secure dividends, strong balance sheets and companies with high and stable 
margins. In Europe, we would include Telecoms and Utilities. 
Investment focus: Selective beneficiaries of lower oil prices and bond 
yields 
Some normalisation in the Middle East and a recovery in Persian Gulf supply should 
reduce geopolitical risk premia and bring down commodity prices, including European 
gas prices. Our commodities team forecast that Brent will fall from around $100/bbl at 
end-2026 to $78/bbl at end-2027, turning the energy-price impulse from a 0.4pp drag 
on global growth into a modest boost in 2027. Combined with fading US tariff effects 
and easing AI-related supply pressures, this should lower inflation and help central 
banks bring it back towards target. The Fed and ECB should move towards rate cuts in 
late 2027, supporting modestly lower bond yields. Our rates forecasts remain more 
dovish than market pricing.  
Investment implications. Lower inflation and bond yields should support long-duration 
Growth and rate-sensitive sectors. Falling energy costs should strengthen consumers’

### Page 15

real incomes across regions and support corporate margins outside the Energy sector. 
Europe should be the clearest beneficiary because of its greater exposure to

exposure, including Consumer Discretionary and long-duration Growth. In Asia, we 
would target selective long-duration and rate-sensitive beneficiaries. In Europe, we 
favour Telecoms, Utilities, UK Real Estate, the DAX and Retailers. 
Overweight the US  
While the US equity market outperformed consistently over the 15 years post the 
financial crisis, its relative performance has faded since 2025. Over the course of 2026, 
the US equity market has seen more of a valuation squeeze than other markets; its ROE 
has increased relative to the rest of the world, while its relative P/B has contracted 
(Exhibit 17). Overall, we see US equity valuations as attractive versus history and also 
compared with other markets when adjusted for growth and likely volatility. We are 
underweight Europe; while lower oil prices would provide an important boost for 
European economies, it would also have a negative impact on earnings growth. 
Valuations have not fallen as much as in other regions, and the risks are higher given 
government debt issues and the heavy political calendar.

The devaluation of emerging market equities has been particularly notable through 
2026 (Exhibit 18). Driven by the AI investment boom, outsized gains in Tech Hardware 
stocks have pushed EM index concentration to record highs — Korea and Taiwan now 
account for about half of the MSCI EM, with Info Tech at >40% and the top 3 stocks (the 
‘AI trio’ of TSMC, Samsung Electronics and SK Hynix) at ~30%. Although the recent 
sell-off leaves room for a potential strong recovery, the risks remain higher than for the 
Hyperscalers. For MXAPJ (incl. Korea/Taiwan), we forecast a 17% USD price return and 
20% total return, supported by 26%/13% EPS growth in 2027/28 and a 9.3x target P/E, 
but Ex-Korea/Taiwan, we forecast 12% USD price return and 15% total return, and

across MXAPJ markets, the median USD total return is 13%, with an average of 15%. 
Given YTD annualised volatility of 24.2% for MXAPJ we recommend a neutral overall

What are the risks? 
We see four in particular: bond volatility, oil volatility, weaker AI capex, and momentum 
concentration — each of which could generate both downside as well as upside risks for 
investors.  
1. The bond market 
The rise in bond yields through 2026 has, so far, largely been offset in equity markets by 
strong earnings growth. That said, it has contributed to lower P/E ratios, and the 
underperformance of sectors that are most sensitive to interest rates, such as Real 
Estate and Defensives, while contributing to the relative strength (until recently) of 
Cyclicals.   
Any relief in bond markets, leading to lower yields, would hamper the relative 
performance of Cyclicals and Financials and favour some of the more defensive 
areas of the equity markets — another reason to have some exposure to selective 
defensives and also sectors that are highly negatively correlated with bond yields.  
Sovereign debt concerns could result in a further spike in bond yields, with the risks 
highest in Europe during an election-heavy year, while an alleviation of inflationary 
pressures might lead to a rally.  
2. Oil prices  
The combination of rising oil prices and bond yields has been central to the movement in 
equity markets through 2026. Our commodity analysts forecast oil at $88/bbl for Brent

Exhibit 18: EM devalued sharply through 2026 compared with DM

### Page 17

and $78/bbl for WTI in 2027, with risks in both directions. In general, lower energy
prices would be good for equity markets, bringing down inflation and interest rate
expectations. On balance, we think this would boost longer duration growth valuations
(from the relatively depressed levels currently), while also supporting defensive growth.
By contrast, such an outcome would reduce profit growth expectations for Energy
companies — a factor that would likely weigh more on Europe given that Energy is a

relatively important contributor to earnings growth.
3. AI capex spend
AI capex spending has been an important support for equity markets over the past three
years, driving earnings growth across the Technology ecosystem, and beyond into the
capex beneficiaries. Our economists expect the AI capex boom to slow. This could lead
to a de-rating in sectors that have become the beneficiaries of the capex boom,
including parts of the Industrials sector, which has re-rated, but also memory and chips

stocks. The latter have de-rated sharply, making the entry point more attractive, but
volatility is likely to remain high.
4. Narrow Breadth & Concentration
Related to this point, the US and Asia equity markets have a high degree of stock
concentration in their indices. Narrow breadth can create more stock volatility, with the
dominant stocks rising or the rest of the market falling. Either way, the momentum factor
risks high volatility and having some diversification across Growth and some Defensives

should help to improve risk-adjusted returns.  From a geographic perspective, the two
most concentrated regions are the US and Asia. That said, given recent market action,
we think the large cap technology names in the US are more defensive that those in Asia.
On a risk-adjusted basis, therefore, we think the momentum and concentration risks are
higher in Asia and imply lower risk-adjusted returns.

## Document 442: What lessons can we learn to keep European industry competitive from the measures taken by Germany, as the cost of electricity continues to turn the screw_ (2026-10-22)

Source PDF: all report pdf/What lessons can we learn to keep European industry competitive from the measures taken by Germany, as the cost of electricity continues to turn the screw_ (2026-10-22).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-22
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
WHAT LESSONS CAN WE LEARN TO KEEP EUROPEAN INDUSTRY COMPETITIVE FROM
THE MEASURES TAKEN BY GERMANY, AS THE COST OF ELECTRICITY CONTINUES TO
TURN THE SCREW?
Marianne Mueller and Pascal Devaux
issue 19
21 October 2026
The 2022 energy crisis clearly highlighted the issue of electricity prices in Europe — and, more broadly, the issue of energy prices. As
a result, European manufacturers’ competitiveness has been severely undermined when compared with the United States and China.

By reigniting tensions over electricity prices, the conflict that began in the Middle East in late February 2026 further underscores the
continent’s vulnerability to fossil fuel imports, despite the expansion of renewable energy. Faced with an environment of potential sharp
and sudden price rises, European governments do not have the same room for manoeuvre.
While the additional costs borne by European manufacturers are mainly linked to the share of gas in national electricity mixes, we will

first illustrate that, in Germany, grid operation and maintenance costs, as well as taxation, are also contributing significantly to these
costs.
We will then explore the solutions proposed by the German government under the new European measures regulating state aid to
electricity-intensive industries.
Finally, our estimates will show that, while these measures make Germany more competitive against some European countries, they
do not place it on an equal competitive footing with the United States or China. Nevertheless, Germany has made significant progress

in decarbonising and making its electricity mix more flexible from a European perspective. This should help to ensure that electricity costs
are reduced in the medium term.

### Page 2

2
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoInsight 19 / 21 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
worldWHAT LESSONS CAN WE LEARN TO KEEP EUROPEAN INDUSTRY COMPETITIVE FROM THE MEA- SURES TAKEN BY GERMANY, AS THE COST OF ELECTRICITY CONTINUES TO TURN THE SCREW?
GAS: A KEY FACTOR IN THE ADDITIONAL ELECTRICITY COSTS
FACED BY EUROPEAN INDUSTRY
European manufacturers are still paying significantly higher elec -
tricity costs than their American and Chinese competitors. In 2019,

there was a 30% difference between the prices charged to European
electricity-intensive industries (corresponding to the lower end of the
industrial price range) and the average US industrial price. In 2025,
it stood at nearly 100% according to the IEA (2026) 1, after peaking at
an average of 148% between 2022 and 2023. There is currently a 50%
difference with their Chinese competitors2.
The disruption to Russian gas supplies since 2022 is behind the rising

gas prices, particularly as the EU has had to turn to liquefied natural
gas3 (LNG), which is more expensive than gas transported via pipe -
line and, above all, exposes Europe to global gas market conditions.
The growth of LNG has, in fact, intensified competition between the
major consumption regions, primarily Europe and Asia. European and
Asian prices now tend to move more in tandem (Chart 1). However, in
China, the prevalence of coal — a widely available local resource —

and a more regulated electricity price help to keep industrial prices
lower. At the same time, the United States enjoys plentiful domestic
gas, which is produced at a low cost, driven by the sharp rise in shale
oil and gas production over the past decade. By contrast, since 2022,
Europe has been more exposed to the cost of imported gas (90% of its
total consumption, according to Eurostat), while the United States and
China have enjoyed plentiful domestic resources.

Fluctuations and the level of gas prices are passed on to Eu -
ropean electricity prices via the ‘merit order’ marginal pricing
mechanism, with the wholesale price determined by the margi -
nal cost of the last (and most expensive) electricity source called
upon to meet demand.
As a result, gas plays a decisive role, with gas-fired power sta -
tions setting the price in around 40% of cases in Europe4, with si-
gnificant variations depending on national electricity mixes. It is

close to this average in Germany, below it in Spain (26%) and
France (17%), and above it in Italy (73%) 5 in 2025 6 7. Therefore,
electricity prices are still sensitive to fluctuations in gas prices,
even when electricity is not generated from gas (Chart 2). This
pass-through is particularly pronounced when demand rises
and requires the use of more expensive sources, or when supply
constraints arise (for example, during extreme heatwaves that
reduce wind-power generation or disrupt the cooling of nuclear

power plants).
1 Prices – Electricity 2026 – Analysis – IEA
2 The difference may be even larger for other manufacturers who do not receive the same support as electricity-intensive sectors.
3 Imports of Russian gas (via pipeline and LNG) accounted for around 45% of total European imports in 2021, compared with 14% in Q2 2026 (Bruegel, natural gas imports).
4 ACER, 2026, Key developments in EU electricity and gas markets, 2026 Monitoring report.
5 In Italy, in response to sharply rising gas prices in 2026, the government is currently negotiating a potential option of capping the prices of gas supplied to power stations in

order to limit the rise in the wholesale price of electricity. However, this can only be a temporary general mechanism, with the potentially major cost being borne by public funds
and/or households.
6 Eurelectric, 2026, Power Barometer.
7 EMBER, 2026, Latest energy shock reminds Europe of its risky reliance on gas.
8 Energy shock: 2026 vs. 2022 dashboard – 2nd October 2026 – Economic Studies – BNP Paribas
However, for the time being, the 2026 energy crisis is having a far

smaller effect on electricity prices than in 2022. On the one hand, the
2026 peak reached by the European TTF benchmark is still well below
that recorded in 20228.
In addition, there has been significant progress in decarbonising Eu -
ropean electricity mixes since 2022, which has helped to mitigate, on
average, the pass-through of gas prices to electricity prices.

SOME EUROPEAN COUNTRIES HAVE MANAGED TO STAY
COMPETITIVE, BUT GERMANY IS STILL THE EXCEPTION
Electricity prices partly vary due to countries’ electricity mixes
(Chart 3). Electricity-intensive manufacturers in countries where the
share of fossil energy is high face higher costs (Poland, Italy and Ire -
land) (Chart 4). Conversely, countries with a less carbon-intensive mix

(particularly France and Spain in recent times) are less directly ex -
posed to these fluctuations, with costs closer to the United States and
China (Chart 5).
The situation in Germany is paradoxical, as, despite progressively
greening its electricity mix and wholesale electricity prices (averaging
90 EUR/MWh over the 2023–2025 period) and average electricity costs
(across all industrial sectors) in line with the European average, the most
energy-intensive industries face higher costs than the European average

(and even more so compared with international competitors). This is
mainly due to grid costs and a tax burden above EU standards (Chart 6).
9 Eurostat data are used to compare prices with the rest of the EU. However, given that electricity prices are not uniform for businesses at a European level, there may be differences
between Eurostat and national sources. Jäger P., 2025, “Cautionary tale or model for Europe? How member states should react to Germany’s new electricity subsidy”, Hertie School,

Jacques Delors Centre.
10 P. Devaux, 2026, “European Union: low carbon transition and energy sovereignty, a path fraught with obstacles”
In the first half of 2026, for the most electricity-intensive industries,
the difference compared with the European average was 9%, based on
the prices excluding taxes and levies provided by Eurostat. This gap is
mainly due to the cost of accessing the grid. If all taxes and levies are
included, the difference rises to 21% (Chart 7)9.

EUROPE’S APPROACH OF ADOPTING BOTH STRUCTURAL
PROGRAMMES AND TARGETED SUPPORT MEASURES
European programmes to support the low-carbon transition pursue
objectives relating to climate action (reducing greenhouse gas emis -
sions), sovereignty10 (increasing energy self-sufficiency) and competi -
tiveness (reducing the volatility and costs associated with fossil fuels).
The RePowerEU programme, which was launched in 2022 in response
to the energy crisis, primarily aims to reduce imports of Russian gas

and, more generally, to accelerate the low-carbon transition. Although
Europe has reduced its dependence on imports of Russian fossil fuels,
there has still not been enough progress around the energy transition
and cross-border connectivity. This is due to governance constraints

and struggles in implementing the programme in practice within each
Member State11.
State aid is still the main vehicle for this. In response to the 2021–
2022 energy crisis, the European Union temporarily relaxed the rules
on this type of aid in order to support businesses facing rising energy
prices. Any intervention of this kind, provided that it is selective and

could affect trade within the single market, must be authorised in ad -
vance by the European Commission in principle. Since June 2025, the
Clean Industrial Deal State Aid Framework (CISAF) has provided a more
sustainable framework for these kinds of interventions. In particular,
this framework, which runs until the end of 2030, authorises Member
States to grant temporary aid to electricity-intensive businesses to off-
set the cost of electricity. The aid must be targeted and come with

decarbonisation commitments12. Against the backdrop of the crisis in
the Middle East, the temporary METSAF scheme, adopted in April 2026,
helped (in relation to measures falling under the CISAF) to increase the
proportion of additional costs offset by governments from 50% to 70%,
thereby offering additional support to the most at-risk sectors.
GERMANY IS STILL SUFFERING FROM INSUFFICIENT IN-
VESTMENT IN ITS ELECTRICITY NETWORKS, WHICH THE
AID SCHEMES ARE ATTEMPTING TO OFFSET

The issue of electricity costs is a particularly hot issue in Germany,
given the importance of industry to the economy (around 20% of va -
lue added, one of the highest levels in Europe) and its exposure to
international competition. Furthermore, the delay in modernising
electricity infrastructure (only 22.6% of the high-voltage lines planned
for 2025 had actually been completed by that date) is causing bottle -
necks between the production areas in the north and the consump -

11 European Court of Auditors, 2026, “Implementation of the REPowerEU plan must be stepped up”.
12 It applies to the most electricity-intensive activities, which are seeing their competitiveness threatened by international competition, and follows the 4x50 principle: 50% of
electricity consumption is eligible for a subsidy; Of the eligible proportion, the subsidy may not exceed 50% of the wholesale price;
The final wholesale price paid by the company must not be less than €50/MWh;

50% of the aid received must be reinvested in decarbonisation (renewable energy generation, storage and improvements in energy efficiency); Around 90 sectors are covered by this
reform, accounting for approximately 2,000 companies, including in the chemical, steel and paper industries. The scheme, which is strictly regulated, will run for just three years
(from 1st January 2026 to the end of 2028) at an estimated total cost of between EUR 3 and 5 bn, financed by the special Climate and Transformation Fund.

13 Bundesnetzagentur Monitoring Report (2015) and Bundesnetzagentur Monitoring Report (2025). These costs mainly cover the costs of physical infrastructure; operational,
regulatory and compliance costs; and costs associated with using the transmission and distribution network.
14 The Industriestrompreis (industrial electricity price) is the public subsidy mechanism aiming to reduce the price of electricity for some industries.
15 The indirect carbon cost compensation mechanism is a government support measure for electricity-intensive industries, which aims to offset the cost of carbon-emission

allowances for energy producers.
tion centres in the south. As a result, the costs of accessing services
have increased fivefold over 10 years (rising from EUR 1 bn in 2014
to EUR 5.2 bn in 2024 13). At the same time, as we have already seen,
taxation and grid charges are hitting industry hard, with levies on large
consumers far exceeding the European average despite some support
mechanisms being put in place for the most electricity-intensive com-

panies (such as the Bandlastprivileg measure to reduce grid tariffs).
As a result, German industrial production has suffered in particular
due to rising energy prices in recent years, including electricity, with
electricity-intensive industries being especially affected (Chart 8).
In order to cope with these higher electricity costs than the European
average, Germany has been using European support schemes to reduce
the cost of electricity for businesses, particularly since the Merz go -

vernment took office in May 2025. The main scheme — the Industries-
trompreis14, made possible by the CISAF — has been approved by the
European Commission for the 2026–2028 period. While it is less bene-
ficial than the indirect carbon cost (ICC) offsetting included in electri -
city prices15, it has a broader scope, meaning that businesses ineligible
for the ICC can benefit from it. Other measures complement this targe-
ted support by addressing other components of the electricity bill. As a

result, the electricity tax has been set at the European minimum for
manufacturing sectors, while the gas-storage tax has been abolished.
The government is also subsidising grid fees, which should halve them.
Overall, according to our estimates based on 2024 prices, the final
price of electricity for these industries could fall by around a quarter
(to 116 EUR/MWh for industries with annual consumption of between
70 and 150 GWh, compared with a European average of 123 EUR/MWh)

(Chart 9). Germany’s advantage over Central and Eastern European
countries, where electricity costs are generally already higher, looks
set to increase.

However, even when taking all of the subsidies into account, the price
of electricity in Germany would remain higher than the levels seen in
France, Spain or Portugal (and even more so in the United States and
China).
FISCAL MEASURES: LIMITED ROOM FOR MANOEUVRE
There are a number of limitations to these European schemes. For
the country implementing them, lower electricity costs mean that

its industry becomes more competitive against industries from other
EU countries, but this does very little to address the disadvantage that
it faces compared with the United States and China. Therefore, this
measure will unlikely be enough to counteract the loss of market share
seen vis-à-vis the United States and China. In addition, the support
schemes are restrictive (conditional on investment in the low-carbon
transition) and are transitional. Finally, their cost is partly passed on

to the public purse or to consumers who do not receive this support
(in Germany, the cost of the new energy price support measures is
estimated at EUR 14.3 bn in 2026, or 0.3% of GDP16).
Some European manufacturers have proposed abolishing or reducing
the carbon tax as a solution to getting electricity prices down quickly.
In Germany, this tax accounted for 14% of the electricity price in the
first half of 2025, which is higher than the EU average (11%). However,

this proposal is already covered by the indirect carbon cost offsetting
mechanism for electricity-intensive industries exposed to carbon al -
lowance prices. In addition, while adjustments are being made — no -
tably to slow the rate at which allowances are reduced — it seems
highly unlikely that the scheme will be significantly challenged. It is
one of the cornerstones of Europe’s policy to combat climate change,
which has been effective thus far, as shown by the falling greenhouse

gas emissions from industry. More broadly, the gradual introduction of
a carbon border adjustment mechanism (CBAM) should mitigate com-
petition distortions caused by environmental practices17.
IN GERMANY AND THE REST OF EUROPE, HEAVY INVEST -
MENT IS DRIVING UP ELECTRICITY BILLS IN THE SHORT
TERM
In response to the challenge of the low-carbon transition and indus -
trial competitiveness, European governments must strike a balance
between decarbonising the electricity mix and ensuring a supply of

energy that is flexible enough (particularly from controllable sources)
to guarantee stable electricity systems. The integration of an increa -
sing proportion of intermittent renewable energy sources requires
massive investment in transmission and storage infrastructure (statio-
nary batteries). This expenditure, which places a long-term burden on
grid costs, reduces the scope for support schemes (particularly public
ones) to address competitiveness gaps. In Germany, the cost of the

transition is estimated at between EUR 122 bn, according to the go -
vernment (2.8% of GDP), and EUR 200 bn, according to the DIHK18 (4.6%
of GDP), by 2030–2035. If we take the ECB’s assumption19 that govern-
ments will cover around 17% of these investments, the annual public
16 The Industriestrompreis is estimated at around EUR 1.3 bn per year, the subsidy for network charges is projected to amount to EUR 6.5 bn in 2026, the abolition of the gas sur -

charge around EUR 3.4 bn, and the reduction in the electricity tax nearly EUR 3 bn per year. Furthermore, the ICC had cost EUR 2.3 bn in 2024 for costs incurred in 2023.

expenditure burden of the transition in Germany would be between

Germany has already made significant progress in maximising its use

of renewable energy, putting it at the head of the pack in Europe. The
country generates 20% of Europe’s renewable electricity (solar and
wind) and accounts for 30% of installed storage capacity (BESS) 21, far
ahead of other European countries (ranking third globally). However,
despite this momentum, Germany’s dependence on gas has not yet
decreased. As a matter of fact, it is only partially offsetting the planned
closure of coal-fired power stations and the phasing out of nuclear

reactors. Furthermore, the intermittent nature of solar and wind power
could prolong this dependence in the short and medium term. In order
to ensure that the German electricity system is flexible, new invest -
ments in gas-fired power plants, supported by public subsidies, are
planned22.

SUBSIDIES ARE HELPING TO ADDRESS THE EMERGENCY
CAUSED BY ENERGY CRISES WITHOUT RESTORING THE
INTERNATIONAL COMPETITIVENESS OF ENERGY-INTEN-
SIVE INDUSTRIES
In response to the impact of energy crises on industrial competitive -
ness, European governments are finding themselves having to strike a
delicate balance between providing short-term support to industries

exposed to increased international competition and continuing — if
not, accelerating — energy transition policies which will result in hi -
gher costs for electricity-network users for several years to come.
Beyond direct economic support measures, which are difficult to sus -
tain long term, four priority areas are emerging:
1/ Demonstrating flexibility in implementing European directives in
order to factor in national specificities and the differing timeframes

between energy crises (which can lead to irreversible industrial conse-
quences) and reforms (which have a longer-term horizon). The relaxa-
tion of the European carbon tax mechanism 23 appears to be a step
towards this.
2/ Continuing efforts to decarbonise the electricity mix, which are still
the main vehicle for sustainably reducing electricity prices for Euro -
pean industries in the medium and long term.
3/ Optimising the use of electricity networks on the demand side, in

particular through smart grids, a flagship element of the Commission’s
COM (2026) 600 reform presented last July24.
4/ Coordinating European electricity networks in order to harness the
complementarities between national systems.
Article completed on 19 October 2026

產業聚焦降息前的美國汽車產業投資布局
2026/10/30
• 美國傳統車商迎來皮卡改款
週期
• 高油價使油電、電動車需求
提升
• 自駕車、軟體營收增加帶動
車商獲利
美國傳統車商迎來皮卡改款週期，獲利表現有望提升
美國傳統車廠近年轉向生產更多高價卡車和SUV，因部分SUV和皮卡的調整後息稅前利潤率高達20%、超
過其他車型，也使美國新車平均交易價格在9月超過5萬美元。福特與通用汽車即將在2026年年底至2027
年迎來全尺寸皮卡改款與更新計畫，由於在推出新款或新一代卡車後，通常會有2~3年較佳的銷售期，預
估福特和通用汽車在2027年及之後的獲利有機會持續提升。
美國政策轉為支持燃油車發展，但高油價使油電、電動車需求提升

儘管美國政策轉向支持燃油車發展，但自美伊戰爭後油價居高不下，使油電混合車3Q26在美國的市佔率
暴衝至16%，包括豐田、現代等品牌在美國的市占率持續上升。美國3Q26電動車銷量約佔新車總銷量
6.5%，高於2Q26的5.8%，顯示電動車在美國市場正重新站穩腳步。由於油價上升使全球對電動車需求
增加，特斯拉2Q26、3Q26交車量也優於市場預期。
自駕車接受度提升，軟體營收增加帶動車商獲利
美國消費者對自動駕駛的認識與接受度正持續提升，而各車商正積極擴大自駕與軟體訂閱服務布局。通用
汽車預估其OnStar和Super Cruise自駕輔助營收在2026年將突破30億美元，福特汽車的軟體付費訂閱用
戶已達160萬戶。3Q26特斯拉FSD活躍用戶數已達174萬、年增67%，且北美新車FSD採用率超過75%。
相較傳統車商低於10%的硬體銷售利潤率，軟體毛利率高達50~70%，且訂閱制使車商部分營收轉為持續
性收入，將帶動其估值表現。

美國9月份輕型車年化銷售量約為
1,600萬輛，低於市場預估共識的
1,630萬輛。儘管9月初的勞動節
促銷活動使購車回饋及零售銷售
增加，但同時銷售量年減4%也顯
示市場需求正面臨更大挑戰。
今年上半年儘管面臨高利率、高
車價等負面影響，但高收入族群
支撐使美國車市仍具韌性。然而
隨著利率上升、對消費者可負擔
性的懷疑日益增加，市場目前正
逐步將2026年美國輕型車銷售量
預估由1,610萬輛下修至1,560至
1,600萬輛區間。
聯準會在9月升息一碼，使美國
目標利率區間上升至3.75~4%，
且目前市場普遍預期聯準會在今
年12月及2027年上半年將再各升
息一碼。美國約有75~85%新車
購買使用貸款或融資，利率持續
上升將直接推高車貸利率，進而
增加消費者的購車成本與負債壓
力，且利率上升也會造成核貸門
檻轉趨嚴格，使消費者對新車的
需求下降。
未來若油價、通膨下降，市場重

新步入降息循環後有望使美國車
市需求重新回升。資料來源：Cox Automotive，2026/9/24，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10/22

車商將迎來皮卡改款週期
回饋優惠在交易價中佔比美國新車交易價格美國新車平均交易價格( ATP )在
9月超過5萬美元，車價上漲在每

年下半年為常見現象，以因應車
款更新而重新定價。然而，9月
ATP年增率低於1%遠低於典型的
9月增幅2~4%，顯示利率上升後
平價車款銷售相對強勁。
儘管美國新車售價成長幅度趨緩，
但其上升趨勢仍使美國傳統車商
利潤率逐步改善。通用、福特汽
車等傳統車廠近年轉向生產更多
卡車和SUV，因部分SUV和皮卡
的調整後息稅前利潤率超過20%，
使得車廠利潤率能維持一定水準。
(美元)
福特與通用汽車即將在2026年年
底至2027年迎來全尺寸皮卡改款
與 更 新 計 畫 ， 福 特 旗 艦 皮 卡 F-
150將換上全新3.0升V6引擎，通
用汽車則將對GMC Sierra 及
Chevrolet Silverado全尺寸皮卡
進行大改款。
鑑於福特與通用汽車在皮卡銷售
中取得的較高利潤，皮卡市場對
獲利的重要性可想而知。由於原
廠在推出新款或新一代卡車後，
通常會有2~3年較佳的銷售期，
預估福特和通用汽車在2027年及
之後的獲利有機會持續提升。

4
高油價使美國電動車銷售逐步回溫
資料來源：Cox Automotive，2026/10/12，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10/28
需求使油電、電動車並存
康寧儘管政策轉向支持燃油車發展，
但自美伊戰爭後油價居高不下，
使油電混合車3Q26在美國的市佔
率暴衝至16%，包括豐田、現代
等品牌在美國的市占率持續上升。

而儘管美國3Q26電動車銷量較去
年同期下降(3Q25為電動車補助
期限，基期較高)，但仍較2Q26
成長近8%，約佔美國新車總銷量
的6.5%，高於2Q26的5.8%，顯
示電動車在美國市場正重新站穩
腳步。以市場反應來看，美國車
市正從先前單一電動化路線，轉
向燃油、油電、電動車並存局面。
美國國會在2025年9/30 正式取
消購買或租賃新電動車的7,500
美元稅收抵免，造成美國電動車
銷售在4Q25開始大幅下滑。另
外，川普政府在今年9月底正式
宣布放寬美國企業燃油效率標準，
將未來新車的燃油效率年均提升
目標下調，使部分車商開始降低
電動車生產以減少虧損。
傳統車企如福特、通用汽車的電
動車部門EBIT利潤率可能介於-
10~-30%以下，燃油效率標準放
寬使其生產方向轉回燃油車發展，
也有助於改善整體獲利結構。
拜登政府標準
(2024年)
川普政府新規
(2026年9月)

軟體營收增加帶動獲利表現
各車商正積極擴大自駕與軟體訂閱
服務布局，並將其視為未來重要獲
利成長動能。 通 用 汽 車 預 估 其
OnStar和Super Cruise自駕輔助營

收在2026年將突破30億美元，並
在未來維持雙位數成長。福特汽車
的軟體付費訂閱用戶已達160萬戶。
3Q26特斯拉FSD活躍用戶數已達
174 萬 、 年增67% ， 且 北 美 新 車
FSD採用率超過75%。
相較傳統車商低於10%的硬體銷售
利潤率，軟體毛利率高達50~70%，
且訂閱制使車商部分營收轉為持續
性收入，將帶動其估值表現。
美國消費者對自動駕駛的認識與接
受度正持續提升。過去十年來，對
自駕車有較多了解的民眾比例由
40%增至58%，且近半數消費者相
信在有生之年將能看到自駕車普及。
另外，消費者雖然支持車輛自動化
發展，但仍傾向保有人工接管能力，
因此對Level 4 的 興 趣 最 高 ， 對
Level 5的興趣反而下降。
調查也發現，多數曾搭乘Robotaxi
的使用者對自動駕駛的觀感明顯改
善，未來隨著服務覆蓋範圍持續擴
大，Robotaxi有望進一步提升大眾
對自駕技術的信心與接受度。 資料來源：Cox Automotive，2026/9/28，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10/29

PCB 概念：涨停潮逆势突围， " 全球短缺 " 警报成直接催化
PCB 是全天题材中持续性最强的⼀条线，在半导体整体杀跌的背景下逆势活跃。
中京电⼦ 2 连板涨停，满坤科技 20cm 涨停，超声电⼦涨停，澳弘电⼦涨停，骏亚科技涨停，东
晶电⼦涨停，光华科技涨停，易天股份 20cm 涨停。不过⼤市值⻰头仍分化⾛弱 —— 沪电股份收

### Page 5

跌 0.96% ，东⼭精密跌 5.72% 。
催化逻辑清晰。最直接的是全球电⼦协会（ Global Electronics Association ） 11 ⽉ 19 ⽇发布的供
应链警报。该协会指出 " 全球电⼦产业正⾯临印刷电路板（ PCB ）的严重短缺 "" 真正制约供应的
是上游环节，包括玻璃纤维、树脂、铜箔以及⽣产设备 " ，并判断供应瓶颈 " ⾄少持续到 2027 年上
半年、 2028 年年中之前难以实质性缓解 " 。
申港证券指出， "PCB 产业链供需紧张蔓延，缺料从载板扩及设备与原料。 AI 算⼒增速 2024 年以
来约每五个⽉翻倍，预计 2028 年 AI 加速芯⽚（ XPU ）需求规模相较 2024 年将达六倍，但⾼阶载
板、存储与先进封装产能同期仅增⾄约两倍， 2027 年 PCB 载板材料缺⼝约 20%" 。
东吴证券认为， " 头部板⼚再出扩产计划， PCB ⼚资本开⽀有望持续超预期 " ，现阶段扩产⽅向均
为 mSAP ⼯艺类载板或 HDI 板、 " 设备端最为受益 " 。

鑫科技跌 6.24% 、兆易创新跌 5.91% 、澜起科技跌 6.68% 。唯中芯国际逆势收涨 2.63% ，寒武纪

4. 11/18 現貨⾦價跌⾄ 2025 年 9 ⽉來低點，倫敦銅創 4 ⽉來新低

6. 11/18 美國原油庫存意外增加 338 萬桶， WTI 結算 82.31 美元
7. 11/18 騰訊第三季營收年增 8% ，⾃由現⾦流負 691 億元
8. 11/18 習近平深圳迎接川普，中美俄三⽅會晤預計 11/19 舉⾏
9. 11/18 ⽇本 10 ⽉出⼝年增 20.5% ，創單⽉出⼝⾦額新⾼
10. 11/18 外資賣超台股 765.6 億元，三⽇累計賣超 1,848 億元
事件  1
11/18 Fed ⼗⽉會議紀要：幾乎所有官員認為需進⼀步升息
五⼤重點
1  Fed 於美東 11/18 公布 10 ⽉ 27 ⾄ 28 ⽇會議紀要，當時以 10 ⽐ 2 維持利率 3.75% ⾄ 4.00% ，理事庫
克與克⾥夫蘭 Fed 總裁哈瑪克投反對票，主張升息 1 碼。
2   紀要寫明「幾乎所有」與會者認為，若經濟如預期發展，進⼀步升息可能是適當的；有兩位與
會者提出，若通膨數據不利， 12 ⽉可考慮升息超過 1 碼。

### Page 2

3   「絕⼤多數」與會者認為通膨⾵險偏上⾏，原因是能源價格偏⾼與成品油供應受限；多位與會
者指出 AI 建置需求正推升科技產品價格，是重要的通膨⾵險來源。
4   幾乎所有與會者討論 AI 報酬可能不如預期，多數指出 AI 相關債務以複雜且不透明的結構快速增
加；紀要公布後 2 年期公債殖利率升⾄ 4.80% ， S&P 500 收跌 0.24% 。
5  Fed 幕僚預估明年通膨隨能源回落⽽明顯下降、 2028 年回到約 2% ，失業率到 2029 年都低於⻑
期估計值；紀要稱就業市場穩定、接近充分就業。
三⼤結論
1   紀要確認 10 ⽉暫停是時機選擇⽽⾮政策轉向，與美國 10 ⽉核⼼ CPI ⽉增 0.30% 、⾮農新增 16.5
萬⼀致， 12 ⽉升息 1 碼仍是基準；⻑端殖利率維持 5% 以上，⻑久期股票與⻑債的折現壓⼒未解
除。
2  Fed 把 AI 相關債務列為⾦融穩定議題，與⾼槓桿新雲端以外部融資擴產的⾵險相互印證；評價
仍應區分有⾃由現⾦流的上游與依賴再融資的中游，不宜把 AI 供應鏈視為同⼀部位。

3   當 12 ⽉前公布的核⼼服務價格與房租再加速、市場開始定價 2 碼時，應維持短天期國庫券為利
率⾵險核⼼並延後增加⻑債；若核⼼⽉增回落且標售需求改善，再逐步延⻑存續期間。
事件  2
11/18 Fed 理事華勒主張 12 ⽉升息 2 碼，巴爾與戴利也⽀持續升息
五⼤重點
1  Fed 理事華勒 11/18 接受 CNBC 訪問表⽰，若 Fed 不明確釋出 1 ⽉再升息的訊號， 12 ⽉就應升息 2
碼；他認為只說「保留可能」不會影響市場定價。
2   華勒說明，通膨已連續近六年⾼於 2% ⽬標且正在擴散， Fed 需要收回去年的三次降息；紐約
Fed 總裁威廉斯前⼀⽇（ 11/17 ）則表⽰情勢不需要劇烈⾏動。
3  Fed 理事巴爾同⽇演說指出，通膨過⾼、正在壓迫許多家庭，⽽經濟強勁、失業率低、消費穩
健，若經濟如預期發展，他可能⽀持進⼀步緊縮。
4   舊⾦⼭ Fed 總裁戴利同⽇表⽰仍需升息使通膨回到 2% ，但應謹慎、漸進；她指出所得分布下半
部家庭的消費仍有韌性，這既是優勢也是⾵險。

5   市場仍未接受 2 碼情境：利率預測市場對 12 ⽉⼤幅升息的機率僅約 9% ；美元指數升⾄約
103.8 ，為 2025 年 4 ⽉以來⾼點。

### Page 3

三⼤結論
1  Fed 內部分歧已從「是否升息」轉為「升息幅度與節奏」，華勒的 2 碼主張與紀要中兩位與會者
的看法⼀致， 12 ⽉會議前的通膨數據將決定是 1 碼加前瞻指引，還是⼀次 2 碼。
2   鷹派發⾔推升美元並壓抑貴⾦屬，印證短線美元流動性與短券的相對優勢；但官員對節奏仍有
歧⾒，不宜把單⼀官員主張直接視為 Fed 決議。
3   當 12 ⽉會議前的核⼼ CPI 與房租外服務續強、利率期貨定價 2 碼機率明顯升⾼時，應降低⻑久
期與⾼估值成⻑股權重、保留短券；若核⼼降溫使官員回到漸進路徑，再分批回補品質股。
事件  3
11/18 Anthropic IPO 定價每股 83 美元，⾼於原區間，估值約 1.5 兆美元
五⼤重點
1  Anthropic 於美東 11/18 確定 IPO 價格為每股 83 美元，⾼於原定 70 ⾄ 78 美元區間，完全稀釋後估
值約 1.5 兆美元， 11/19 起在那斯達克以代號 ANT 交易。
2   公司原計畫出售約 8.25 億股，因需求強勁擴⼤發⾏規模但未公布最終股數；以原股數計算募資

約 685 億美元（推算），另有科技企業認購 260 億美元無表決權股份。
3   估值⾼於 5 ⽉私募輪的 9,650 億美元，但低於數⽉前傳出的 2 兆美元⽬標；知情⼈⼠指出機構認
購意向約 4,000 億美元、散戶約 1,000 億美元。
4   定價前⼀⽇科技股連續震盪， Cboe 預計最快在掛牌後第⼆個交易⽇推出選擇權；七位共同創
辦⼈各出售近 340 萬股。
5   招股⽂件顯⽰今年上半年營收約為去年同期 13 倍、第⼆季⾸度單季營業獲利；但 Token 降價與
企業控管 AI 預算，仍是收⼊成⻑的主要變數。
三⼤結論
1   定價⾼於區間代表資本市場仍願意為具收⼊成⻑的模型企業提供⼤規模資⾦， AI 資本⽀出的融
資管道暫未收縮；但 1.5 兆美元估值已先反映⾼成⻑，上市後股價對季度收⼊更敏感。
2   模型企業的價值取決於⽤量成⻑能否⼤於 Token 降價、企業預算是否擴張，以及新商業模式能
否放量；年化收⼊與已實現收⼊、總額與淨額認列需分開檢視，不宜以 IPO 熱度推論所有 AI 軟體

受惠。

### Page 4

3   當 Anthropic 上市後⾸份季報顯⽰付費⽤量與⽑利同步改善時，應維持有現⾦流的上游與優質
軟體配置；若收⼊成⻑放緩且股價跌破發⾏價，應降低⾼估值 AI 軟體與⾼槓桿雲端曝險。
事件  4
11/18 現貨⾦價跌⾄ 2025 年 9 ⽉來低點，倫敦銅創 4 ⽉來新低
五⼤重點
1   現貨⿈⾦ 11/18 跌約 3.27% ⾄每盎司約 3,722.6 美元，為 2025 年 9 ⽉以來最低； COMEX 12 ⽉期
⾦結算 3,741.9 美元，現貨⽩銀跌 4.95% ⾄ 44.93 美元。
2   ⾦屬賣壓主因是美元與升息預期：美元指數升⾄約 103.8 ，為 2025 年 4 ⽉以來⾼點， Fed 紀要與
官員鷹派發⾔強化進⼀步升息的預期，美伊緊張也未引發避險買盤。
3   倫敦⾦屬交易所三個⽉期銅盤中跌逾 6% ⾄每噸 12,430 美元，收跌 756 美元⾄ 12,471 美元；
COMEX 期銅同步創 7 個⽉低點，為連續第五個交易⽇下跌。
4   銅價下跌來⾃投機部位撤出與程式交易，價格跌破技術⽀撐後賣壓擴⼤；資⾦成本上升壓抑⾵

險偏好， AI 資料中⼼需求題材在科技股⽌穩下也未能撐住銅價。
5   上海期貨交易所夜盤同步重挫：滬⾦收跌 2.30% 、滬銀收跌 4.23% 、國際銅收跌 4.59% ；⿈⾦
在 11/6 ⾄ 13 ⽇已下跌 4.60% ，本次為再⼀次加速下跌。
三⼤結論
1   ⿈⾦短線受美元與實質利率同步⾛升壓制，事件期未發揮避險功能，與先前下修⿈⾦短期評分
⽅向⼀致；⿈⾦仍保留分散財政與貨幣信⽤尾端的功能，但不宜作為唯⼀避險。
2   銅價跌破關鍵價位反映中國傳統需求不⾜與資⾦⾯收緊，電網與 AI 需求雖在，但無法抵銷投機
部位撤出；銅宜分批配置，避免把 AI 題材視為價格⽀撐的保證。
3   當核⼼通膨回落、美元與實質利率停⽌同步上升時，應逐步回補⿈⾦分散部位；若庫存累積⽽
電網與 AI 訂單放慢，應下修銅的配置，⽩銀維持⼩型衛星不追反彈。
事件  5
11/18 ECB 副總裁武伊契奇稱 12 ⽉升息可能性⾮常⼤
五⼤重點

### Page 5

1   歐洲央⾏副總裁武伊契奇 11/18 表⽰，依⽬前情況 12 ⽉升息的可能性⾮常⼤，薪資與物價的⼆
次傳導效應在 2027 年「肯定」會出現； ECB 存款機制利率⽬前為 2.50% 。
2   同⽇歐盟統計局公布 10 ⽉ HICP （調和消費者物價指數）終值年增 4.0% ，與初值相同，創 2023
年以來新⾼；能源價格年增約 21% ，是主要推升⼒量。
3  10 ⽉核⼼ HICP 終值年增 2.6% ，⾼於公布前預期的 2.5% （預期以初值為基礎形成）； HICP ⽉增
0.5% ，也⾼於預期 0.4% ，顯⽰能源以外的價格壓⼒略升。
4   武伊契奇同⽇發布⾦融穩定評估，指出 AI 估值建⽴在⾼獲利預期上， AI 基礎建設愈來愈依賴舉
債，市場情緒轉向時虧損可能由股市傳導⾄債市。
5   歐元兌美元 11/18 跌⾄約 1.0965 ，創 19 個⽉新低；知情⼈⼠稱 ECB 將於 11/25 討論把銀⾏最低準
備⾦率由 1% 提⾼⾄ 2% ，決定可能延⾄ 12 ⽉。
三⼤結論

1   核⼼ HICP ⾼於預期與副總裁的明確表態，使 12 ⽉升息由研究基準變得更接近政策共識；但能
源仍是主要推升因素，薪資成⻑已降⾄ 3.0% ，⼆次傳導是 2027 年的⾵險⽽⾮已發⽣的事實。
2   升息與能源成本同時壓抑企業回報，法國財政⾵險仍在，歐股維持中性偏審慎，偏好低負債、
具海外收⼊與定價能⼒者；歐元的利差⽀撐被能源進⼝與成⻑落差抵銷，維持中性偏低配置。
3   當 12 ⽉確定升息且核⼼ HICP 續升、薪資重新加速時，應降低歐洲週期股與歐元曝險；若能源
回落使總體通膨下降、 PMI 廣度改善，再考慮增加歐洲週期性部位。
事件  6
11/18 美國原油庫存意外增加 338 萬桶， WTI 結算 82.31 美元
五⼤重點
1  EIA （美國能源資訊署） 11/18 公布上週原油庫存增加 338 萬桶，市場預估僅增加約 5.8 萬桶；汽
油庫存增加 519 萬桶，餾分油增加 157 萬桶，增幅皆⾼於預期。
2  EIA 資料同時顯⽰美國週度原油產量連續第四週創新⾼，東岸煉油廠開⼯率升⾄ 2023 年 1 ⽉以

來最⾼，供給⾯改善使油價承壓。
3  WTI 12 ⽉期貨盤中跌逾 4% ⾄約 81.3 美元，結算 82.31 美元；布蘭特收在約 89.17 美元， 11/17 收
盤為 93.14 美元，單⽇跌幅約 4.3% （推算，合約⽉份不同）。

### Page 6

4   美國國防部⻑赫格塞思同⽇突訪卡拉卡斯，與委內瑞拉簽署軍事合作協議；他稱雙⽅⽯油協議
使荷莫茲海峽成為「⾮關鍵因素」，但未公布供油量等細節。
5   跌幅受荷莫茲海峽航運攻擊限制： 11/17 有船隻在海峽遭襲擊、 1 名船員失蹤；美國國家情報總
監同⽇表⽰來⾃伊朗的威脅已⼤幅升⾼。
三⼤結論
1   美國庫存與產量雙增顯⽰實體供給正在修復，油價已遠離 10 ⽉布蘭特 116 美元⾼點；能源通膨
回落有助緩和整體物價，但對核⼼服務通膨的影響需數⽉才會反映。
2   美委協議缺乏供油量細節，荷莫茲航運攻擊仍在，地緣溢價可能反覆；原油宜維持⼩型事件部
位，能源股保留低成本、低負債⽣產商，不宜以單⽇跌幅追空。
3   當美伊和談維持、美國庫存連續累積且裂解價差回落時，應降低能源類股戰術權重；若荷莫茲
航運中斷再擴⼤、庫存轉降，才重新提⾼原油避險部位。
事件  7
11/18 騰訊第三季營收年增 8% ，⾃由現⾦流負 691 億元
五⼤重點
1   騰訊 11/18 港股收盤後公布第三季營收 2,073.4 億元⼈⺠幣，年增 8% ，低於市場預估 2,105.3 億

元；營業利益 591.5 億元，年減 7% ，低於預估 665.9 億元。
2   第三季資本⽀出付款 651 億元，年增 225% ；⾃由現⾦流為負 691 億元，公司說明主因是預付算
⼒採購款，排除後⾃由現⾦流為正 169 億元。
3   排除新 AI 產品的營業利益為 833 億元、年增 8% ，利潤率維持 40% ；公司歸屬淨利 636 億元，年
增 0.8% ，⾮國際會計準則淨利 665 億元，年減 6% 。
4   雲端收⼊年增 35% ，管理層歸因於 WorkBuddy 、模型即服務與 GPU 租賃需求， WorkBuddy ⽇
活躍⽤戶逾 1,000 萬；⾏銷服務收⼊年增 19% ⾄ 430 億元。
5   傳統業務放緩：國際遊戲收⼊年減 9% ⾄ 189 億元，社交網路收⼊年減 1% ⾄ 318 億元；財報公布
後主要股東 Prosus 股價下跌 5.9% 。
三⼤結論
1   騰訊與美國雲端巨頭同樣出現「 AI 收⼊加速、⾃由現⾦流轉負」，顯⽰ AI 投資與營收認列的時

間差已擴及中國平台；評價重點應放在雲端收⼊能否覆蓋算⼒預付款與折舊。

### Page 7

2   營收低於預期且社交與國際遊戲轉弱，呼應中國 10 ⽉零售僅年增 0.4% 的內需疲弱；中國股市
宜維持選擇性衛星部位，內需平台不宜全⾯提⾼權重。
3   當騰訊後續季度的算⼒預付款轉為雲端收⼊、⾃由現⾦流回正，且中國零售與⺠間投資連續改
善時，才考慮提⾼中國網路平台權重；若資本⽀出續增⽽營收維持個位數成⻑，應維持低配。
事件  8
11/18 習近平深圳迎接川普，中美俄三⽅會晤預計 11/19 舉⾏
五⼤重點
1   中國國家主席習近平 11/18 下午在深圳寶安機場親⾃迎接出席 APEC 領導⼈⾮正式會議的美國總
統川普，以回應 9 ⽉訪美時川普夫婦的機場迎接禮遇。
2   習近平表⽰期待就中美關係、亞太合作與國際情勢深⼊交流，建⽴以尊重、公平、互惠為基礎
的建設性戰略穩定關係；川普稱此⾏將推進雙邊關係並取得圓滿成功。
3   克⾥姆林宮 11/14 宣布，普丁、習近平與川普將於 11/19 在深圳舉⾏三⽅會晤，議程尚未公布；
俄⽅稱普丁與川普 11/18 未進⾏實質會談。
4   中國國台辦 11/18 表⽰台灣問題純屬中國內政，三⽅會晤資訊將適時發布；美國眾議院另⼀委

員會同⽇致函輝達暫代執⾏⻑，要求說明晶⽚出⼝情況。
5   會晤前背景：美國商務部 11/16 已將受限制實體使⽤遠端雲端算⼒納⼊出⼝管制；三⽅會晤結
果與任何晶⽚、關稅協議截⾄發稿均尚未公布。
三⼤結論
1   ⾼規格接待顯⽰中美雙⽅有意穩定關係，但截⾄⽬前沒有公布任何具體協議，出⼝管制規則仍
有效；在正式⽂件公布前，不宜預先推算對 AI 晶⽚海外收⼊的影響。
2   ⼈⺠幣與中國股市應分開判讀：境內美元供給偏多⽀撐⼈⺠幣，但內需仍弱，⼈⾏已提⾼外匯
存款準備⾦率抑制單向升值；峰會氣氛本⾝不⾜以構成全⾯提⾼中國資產權重的理由。
3   當會晤後公布的正式⽂件涉及晶⽚出⼝許可或關稅調整時，應逐項核對適⽤範圍與⽣效⽇，再
調整半導體與中國出⼝技術股權重；若僅有原則性聲明，維持既有配置。
事件  9
11/18 ⽇本 10 ⽉出⼝年增 20.5% ，創單⽉出⼝⾦額新⾼

### Page 8

五⼤重點
1   ⽇本財務省 11/18 公布 10 ⽉出⼝年增 20.5% ，略⾼於市場預估 20% ，出⼝⾦額約 11.8 兆⽇圓，創
單⽉歷史新⾼，已連續 14 個⽉成⻑； 9 ⽉為年增 16.5% 。
2  10 ⽉進⼝年增 21.6% ，低於預估 25.8% ，⾦額約 12.2 兆⽇圓，同樣創單⽉新⾼，但進⼝數量年
減 0.2% ；貿易逆差 4,765 億⽇圓，連續第 6 個⽉逆差，⼩於預估的 9,560 億⽇圓。
3   出⼝成⻑由 AI 投資帶動的半導體與晶⽚製造設備出貨⽀撐；對美國出⼝年增 23.9% ，對中國年
增 13.3% ，對歐盟年增 22.1% 。
4   進⼝⾦額上升主要反映原油價格與運費偏⾼，中東航運受阻使⽇本 10 ⽉⾃美國進⼝⽯油⾦額⼤
增；能源成本是貿易持續逆差的主因。
5   背景：⽇本 11/16 公布第三季 GDP 意外萎縮；⽇銀總裁植⽥和男預定 11/19 上午出席國會，市場
關注其對 12 ⽉升息的說法。
三⼤結論
1   ⽇本外需在 AI 設備與半導體帶動下持續強勁，與第三季 GDP 萎縮、家庭實質消費疲弱形成

「外熱內冷」；企業盈利仍有⽀撐，⽇股宜偏重設備、材料與具現⾦流的企業。
2   能源進⼝推升逆差，弱⽇圓提⾼進⼝成本、強⽇圓則壓縮出⼝收⼊，匯率不能單向視為利多；
⽇銀下次升息研究情境仍為 12 ⽉或明年 1 ⽉，但內需疲弱限制升息速度。
3   當⽇本實質消費改善、⽇銀明確釋出 12 ⽉升息訊號時，可分批提⾼⽇股與⽇圓部位；若內需
持續萎縮使升息延後，應維持⽇股選股⽽⾮指數全⾯加碼，⽇本⻑債續維持低配。
事件  10
11/18 外資賣超台股 765.6 億元，三⽇累計賣超 1,848 億元
五⼤重點
1   台股加權指數 11/18 收 40,194.91 點，上漲 102.80 點、漲幅 0.26% ，成交⾦額約 1.03 兆元；櫃買
指數下跌 0.31% 。
2   外資及陸資當⽇賣超 765.64 億元，三⽇累計賣超 1,848.47 億元；投信買超 175.58 億元，⾃營商
賣超 41.92 億元，三⼤法⼈合計賣超 632.07 億元。
3   台積電收 2,175 元、上漲 5 元，⼤盤在外資⼤幅提款下仍維持⼩漲，主要由投信與內資承接；新

台幣兌美元盤中貶⾄約 32.98 。

### Page 9

4   前⼀⽇（ 11/17 ）台股下跌 947 點收 40,092 點；外資賣超延續 11/16 單⽇重挫 6.03% 以來的去槓
桿賣壓。
5   ⾼盛 11/17 將台股評等由「加碼」降為「中⽴」，加權指數⽬標由 57,500 點下調⾄ 50,000 點；台
灣 10 ⽉外銷訂單預定 11/20 公布，研究預估 1,125 ⾄ 1,159 億美元。
三⼤結論
1   外資連續⼤額賣超反映 AI 評價與資⾦⾯的重新定價，但台灣 10 ⽉出⼝年增 58.9% 、台積電 10 ⽉
營收年增 59.7% ，顯⽰基本⾯交付仍在；槓桿調整不等於訂單崩潰，也不保證估值已⾒底。
2   外資匯出與美國升息預期同時壓抑新台幣，股市基本⾯與匯率應分開管理；台股以現⾦流較強
的晶圓代⼯為核⼼， ODM 與⾼估值中游需檢查客戶融資、應收與⽑利。
3   當 11/20 外銷訂單符合或⾼於預估、外資賣超收斂且融資維持率回穩時，可分批增持晶圓代⼯
與先進封裝；若訂單低於預估且回款轉弱，應降低 ODM 與⾼槓桿部位，並提⾼新台幣以外的流

動性。
待確認
1. 推算值：事件 3 以原計畫 8.25 億股乘以 83 美元推算募資約 685 億美元（實際發⾏股數已擴⼤但未公布）；事件 6 布蘭
特單⽇跌幅約 4.3% 為不同合約⽉份推算。
2. 單⼀或⼆⼿來源：事件 2 利率預測市場 12 ⽉⼤幅升息機率約 9% ；事件 3 機構與散戶認購意向、招股⽂件營收倍數與
獲利數字取⾃媒體轉述，未直接核對 SEC 原⽂；事件 5 ECB 將討論提⾼最低準備⾦率為知情⼈⼠說法；事件 6 赫格塞
思⽯油協議說法未⾒官⽅⽂件；事件 9 市場預估值取⾃媒體。
3. 數據⼝徑：事件 7 資本⽀出採公司公布的「資本⽀出付款」 651 億元，快訊另有資本⽀出 814.3 億元的不同⼝徑，未
採⽤；事件 9 ⽇本 9 ⽉出⼝年增 16.5% 為最新修正值，與先前公布的 16.7% 不同。

9~10 月全球非投等債指數出現一波跌勢，尤其 CCC 評等信用利差更是大幅擴
大，創下近四年新高。高利率環境持續，非投等債投資應偏向高品質券種。

重摘要

■ 高利率環境已衝擊非投等債企業違約
率，信用利差逐步走升，高盛預期2027
年美國、歐洲非投等債違約將分別由
目前的 3.3%和 4.7%分別上升至 4%
和 5%。

■ 槓桿貸款融資壓力同步走升，尤其軟
體、科技服務為主要重災區，反應目前
Neocloud 融資風險以及傳統軟體業
面臨 AI 的衝擊，由於槓桿貸款與非投
等債高度連動，恐加重非投等債賣壓。。

■ 10 月聯準會雖暫停升息，然而油價攀
升風險加上去年同期低基期，恐導致
未來通膨數據進一步走揚，迫使聯準
會態度再次趨向鷹派，不排除殖利率
再次站回 5%之上，持續對非投等債造
成壓力。

重點摘要 
高利率環境下，非投等債投資避開低評級債券

### Page 3

2026

02-8751-6665 
 www.fubon.com 
臺北市大安區仁愛路四段 169 號

▋ 非投等信用利差仍有上升空間 
儘管美國經濟數據透露出整體景氣依然穩
健，但部分債務市場已經在向市場釋出警訊，
財務體質弱小的企業違約率正在大幅飆升。

9~10 月全球非投等債指數跌幅一度超過
3.6%，相較過去 30 年平均月漲幅 0.6%而
言，這波跌勢幅度並不輕，尤其 CCC 評級
信用利差(OAS)已飆升至 1,200bp，背後隱
含 5 年內違約率超過 60%。雖然在此期間，
非投等債中信評相對較高的 BB 信用利差僅
攀升至約 220bp，仍在相對低位，但若用
2022 年聯準會啟動升息循環，市場利率一
路飆升的環境觀察，對比當前市場預期聯準
會未來仍有 3 碼升息空間，不排除 BB 級評
等利差有進一步上升可能。(圖二)

9 月美國非投等債違約率 3.3%，歐洲為
4.7%(已高於 1999 年以來平均約 4%違約
率)，高盛預估至 2027 年底，兩地區違約率
將分別上升至 4%和 5%，背後假設是美債
十年殖利率維持在目前 5%高檔，若利率攀
升，違約壓力將進一步擴大。

圖二： CCC 信用利差已飆升至警報風險

資料來源：Bloomberg, 10/28/2026 
▋ 槓桿貸款市場違約風險攀升 
儘管2027 年非投等債市場的再融資壓力約
160 億 美 元 ， 規 模 不 大 ， 然 而 進 入
2028~2029 年後將出現明顯壓力，再融資
規模預期將暴增至 1,200 億、2,600 億美元。

市場對於槓桿貸款(金融機構向高負債、信
用評級較低的企業所發放的一種商業貸款)
的不信任度也正在攀升，2028~2029 年到
期的槓桿貸款價格低於 80 美分的比重突破
至 20%之上，代表投資人對於借款企業的
償債能力擔憂，其中又以軟體、科技服務業
占比 32%為主， Neocloud 融資困境和 AI
衝擊傳統軟體業的壓力正在蔓延，由於槓桿
貸款與非投等債市場高度連動，非投等債可
能被同步拖累。(圖三)

此外，2026 年美國 5 大 CSP 資本支出預計
達 8,000 億美元，預期 2027 年將進一步擴
大至 1.2 兆美元，資金排擠效應也讓信評較
差的非投等債企業在籌資上更顯困難。

圖三：市場對於槓桿貸款違約預期正在上升

資料來源：GS, 10/9/2026 *橫軸為貸款到期日

### Page 4

2026

02-8751-6665 
 www.fubon.com 
臺北市大安區仁愛路四段 169 號

▋ 聯準會暫停升息，然油價成隱憂 
殖利率未來是否走揚關鍵還是在聯準會對
於未來利率前景的展望。10 月聯準會剛結
束最新的利率決議，以 10 票贊成、2 票反
對通過維持利率 3.75~4%目標不變，整體
態度相較 7 月有 3 名委員支持升息有所緩
解。本次聯準會暫停升息，主要是委員會認
為需要累積更多資訊，才能確認適當的貨幣
政策立場，殖利率小幅向下樂觀反應。

然而，9 月美國 CPI 數據已出現攀升跡象，
同時觀察 2025 年 11~12 月 WTI 油價維持
在 55~60 美元/桶的低檔區間(較 2025 年
10 月平均 60.2 美元走低)，隨著川普揚言
期中選舉後將對伊朗進行新一輪攻擊，油價
可能進一步走揚，對於通膨數據的干擾將延
續，市場對於後續聯準會態度是否還能維持
恐存疑，殖利率不排除有重新回升風險。(圖
四)

此外，成熟國家債務困境依舊難解，儘管短
期美國財政部或持續釋出暫緩增發長債訊
號，然而藉由國庫券週轉融資的方式終究需
要被扭轉，殖利率仍缺乏大幅下行空間。

圖四：油價攀升壓力恐限制聯準會態度

資料來源：Bloomberg, 10/29/2026 
▋ 結論 
高利率環境正在侵蝕美國中小企業的財務
體質，信評在 CCC 及以下評等企業更是首
當其衝，違約機率攀升。此外，AI 基礎建設
融資壓力所導致的資金排擠效應於 2027
年後恐進一步擴大，更不利非投等債的融資
需求，唯一慶幸的是非投等債 2027 年到期
再融資壓力占比僅約 4%，要到 2028 年後
才會明顯攀升，非投等債等待押注雲端服務
商財務體質於 2028 年改善(獲利大幅上升，
抵銷資本支出融資壓力)。高利率環境持續，
非投等債投資應偏向高品質券種。

## Document 638: 国内经济数据解读（2026年10月）：生产边际放缓、内需仍待夯实

Source PDF: all report pdf/国内经济数据解读（2026年10月）：生产边际放缓、内需仍待夯实.pdf
Report source: 浙商期货
Date: 2026-11-17
Pages: 14
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

国内经济数据解读（2026年10月）
生产边际放缓、内需仍待夯实
宏观研究报告
2026-11-16
数据截至10月／1-10月累计
浙商期货有限公司期货交易咨询业务资格：证监许可[2011]1287号
撰写人：朱枭鹏，咨询证号：Z0015158 ｜ 联系电话：0571-87219355
核心判断
观点结论：10月经济延续"生产强、外需旺、内需弱、地产拖累"的分化格局，但生产端边际放缓。规模以上工业增加值同比+4.8%（较9
月回落0.7pct）、服务业生产指数+4.6%；外需仍是最大支撑，10月出口同比+28.7%、进出口总额+31.2%。内需端压力加大：社零同
比仅+0.4%（回落0.7pct）、除汽车外+2.5%，1-10月固投累计-8.1%（降幅扩大0.9pct）、房地产开发投资-21.6%（降幅再扩
1.0pct）、民间投资-11.4%。就业保持稳定，10月城镇调查失业率5.1%（降0.1pct）。整体看，新动能（装备制造+10.6%、高技术制
造+17.6%）仍是核心亮点，但地产链深度调整与内需不足仍是主要制约，经济稳中向好基础还需巩固。

近期驱动：①政策基调延续"更加积极的财政政策+适度宽松的货币政策"，发改委明确及时出台增量政策、推动存量与增量协同发力，"十五
五"规划与"六张网"重大项目加快开工；②8000亿元新型政策性金融工具已于10月28日全部投放完毕（国开3650亿、进出口1200亿、农发
3150亿）；③金融支持服务业增量政策落地，包括降低抵押补充贷款利率、增加再贷款额度、居民住房贷款贴息；④10月末社融存量+7.2%、
M2+7.1%均高于名义GDP，流动性充裕；⑤10月制造业PMI 49.3%重回收缩区间、服务业商务活动指数50.0%，景气边际转弱。
浙商期货宏观研究团队

### Page 2

一、公布数据一览
指标 最新值 前值 变化 初步判断
规模以上工业增加值：当月同比 4.8% 5.5% ↓ 0.7pct 低于预期5.0%，环比仅+0.21%；制造业+5.4%回落，采矿业+0.7%走弱
规模以上工业增加值：累计同比 5.3% 5.3% → 与1-9月持平，累计增速保持平稳；装备/高技术制造支撑
社会消费品零售总额：当月同比 0.4% 1.1% ↓ 0.7pct 低于预期，环比仅+0.02%；以旧换新退坡、汽车拖累延续
社会消费品零售总额：累计同比 1.0% 1.1% ↓ 0.1pct 累计增速继续下探，消费修复动能偏弱、信心待提振
固定资产投资完成额：累计同比 -8.1% -7.2% ↓ 0.9pct 降幅扩大、弱于预期小幅收窄；10月环比-2.93%
固定资产投资完成额：制造业：累计同比 -2.9% -2.1% ↓ 0.8pct 降幅再度走扩，制造业投资转弱；高技术产业投资+4.0%仍正增长
固定资产投资完成额：基础设施建设投资：累计同比 -5.1% -4.2% ↓ 0.9pct 降幅走扩，专项债与政策性金融工具实物工作量尚未充分形成

房地产开发投资完成额：累计同比 -21.6% -20.6% ↓ 1.0pct 降幅连续扩大，地产链仍是最大拖累；新开工-24.4%、竣工-26.6%
城镇调查失业率 5.1% 5.2% ↓ 0.1pct 失业率下行属改善；31个大城市同步降至5.1%，就业总体稳定
注：工业、社零最新值为10月当月/累计，固投及分项、地产为1-10月累计值，城镇调查失业率为10月当月值；前值为上月实际值。数据来源：国家统计局2026-11-16公布。变化列
按红涨绿跌着色（失业率下行属改善但按数值方向着绿，含义见判断列）。
二、分项拆解：工业增加值
10月规模以上工业增加值同比+4.8%，比上月回落0.7个百分点，环比+0.21%，略低于市场预期的5.0%，生产端边际放缓。1-10月累计
+5.3%，与1-9月持平，累计增速仍保持平稳。分三大门类看，制造业+5.4%仍是主力但较9月的6.0%明显回落（采矿业+0.7%、电力热力燃气水
+4.4%）。结构上新动能依旧突出：装备制造业+10.6%、高技术制造业+17.6%，分别快于全部规上工业5.8和12.8个百分点，是生产端最核心的

支撑；3D打印设备（+85.9%）、锂离子电池（+30.0%）、工业机器人（+26.0%）产量维持高增。

### Page 3

边际转弱的信号同样明确：10月制造业PMI回落至49.3%、重回收缩区间，企业生产经营活动预期指数53.4%较前期回落；分经济类型看，国有
控股+2.1%、股份制+5.2%、外商及港澳台+3.0%、私营+3.2%，内资与外资分化明显。1-9月规上工业企业利润总额60337亿元、同比+15.5%，
盈利改善仍是生产的重要支撑，但若需求端持续偏弱，后续生产动能或面临约束。
图1 工业增加值累计同比季节图（2022年至今）

### Page 4

图2 工业增加值三大门类当月同比（近五年）
三、分项拆解：社会消费品零售总额
10月社零同比+0.4%，较上月回落0.7个百分点，环比仅+0.02%，明显弱于预期，消费修复动能进一步走弱；1-10月累计+1.0%继续下探。结
构上呈"服务强于商品、除汽车强于总额"的弱修复：1-10月服务零售额+4.9%、显著快于商品零售+0.9%；10月除汽车以外零售额+2.5%，高于总
额2.1个百分点，汽车仍是最大单品类拖累。限额以上单位消费品零售额-4.9%，与通讯器材+6.5%、文化办公用品+4.1%形成反差，反映大企业与
中小商户分化加剧。

### Page 6

图4 社零及分项当月同比（近五年，汽车类截至9月）
四、分项拆解：固定资产投资
1-10月固定资产投资累计同比-8.1%，降幅较1-9月扩大0.9个百分点，弱于市场"小幅收窄"的预期，10月当月环比-2.93%，投资端压力仍在加
大。三大分项全面走弱：房地产开发投资-21.6%、制造业投资-2.9%、基础设施投资-5.1%，降幅分别扩大1.0、0.8、0.9个百分点。结构亮点仍在
新质生产力方向：高技术产业投资+4.0%、知识产权产品投资+9.2%；但民间投资-11.4%，投资意愿明显不足。政策层面，8000亿元新型政策性
金融工具已于10月28日全部投放，四季度地方债加发与"六张网"开工有望形成更多实物工作量。

### Page 7

图5 固定资产投资完成额累计同比季节图（2022年至今）

### Page 8

图6 三大投资分项累计同比（近五年）
五、分项拆解：房地产
房地产仍未止跌且降幅再度扩大，是需求端最大拖累。1-10月房地产开发投资累计同比-21.6%，降幅较1-9月扩大1.0个百分点。三大施工链条
全面承压：施工面积-13.0%、新开工-24.4%、竣工-26.6%，其中竣工降幅较1-9月明显走扩，反映"保交楼"高峰后竣工动能衰减。销售端继续走
弱，销售面积-13.7%、销售额-14.3%，销售额降幅大于面积，隐含房价同比仍在下跌；待售面积同比-1.7%，小幅去化但仍处高位。资金端压力未
解，到位资金-21.9%。唯一亮点是二手房网签面积+11.4%，"以价换量"下存量消化相对活跃。地产企稳仍需销售端与资金面的实质改善。

六、分项拆解：就业与收入
10月全国城镇调查失业率5.1%，比上月和上年同期均下降0.1个百分点，就业形势总体稳定；31个大城市城镇调查失业率5.1%同步回落。结构
上外来农业户籍失业率4.8%相对最低，农民工就业保持韧性。就业稳定是当前经济的重要"压舱石"，但在社零增速降至0.4%的背景下，就业"量
稳"尚未转化为"收入预期改善"——三季度居民人均可支配收入实际+4.4%，改善幅度温和。后续若稳就业政策带动收入预期回升，消费修复基础将
更扎实。

### Page 12

图10 城镇调查失业率走势（近五年）
七、交叉验证：经济基本面结构亮点与隐忧
10月结构分化进一步强化为供应端边际放缓、需求端内需走弱、外需成为唯一有效对冲：
亮点：①新动能供给强劲，装备制造业与高技术制造业分别快于规上工业5.8和12.8个百分点，是生产端最稳固支撑；②外需强韧，出口高增有效对
冲内需下行；③服务消费与升级类商品较快增长；④高技术产业投资与知识产权产品投资保持正增长，投资端靠新质生产力接棒；⑤就业稳定，为消
费修复提供基础。

### Page 13

隐忧：①地产链条深度拖累且降幅扩大，销售端未止跌；②投资三大分项同步走扩，民间投资意愿明显不足；③消费内生动力不足，汽车类深度负增
长、限额以上单位走弱；④生产端边际转弱，工业增加值回落、制造业PMI重回收缩区间。
交叉验证结论："供强需弱、外强内弱"格局进一步固化，且生产端也开始边际放缓，意味着外需对内的对冲效果正在被削弱。生产端靠新动能支撑、
传统动能走弱；需求端出口强、投资与消费双向走弱。新动能切换进度仍不足以完全熨平地产与内需缺口，逆周期政策的实物工作量形成节奏将决定
四季度能否企稳。整体判断：经济"总体平稳、向新向优"的定性未变，但"稳中向好基础还需巩固"在10月数据中体现得更为充分。
八、综合结论
宏观定位：当前经济处于"总体平稳、动能向新、内需偏弱"的转型阶段，10月数据确认生产端由强转缓、内需端压力加大；1-10月工业
+5.3%、出口+17.6%仍支撑总量，但固投-8.1%、地产-21.6%、社零+1.0%显示内生动力不足。经济稳中向好基础还需巩固，政策需在
扩大内需与地产止跌上加力。

宏观总结：10月经济延续"生产强、出口旺、内需弱、地产拖累"分化格局，但边际上生产端开始放缓（工业+4.8%、较9月回落0.7pct，制造业PMI
回落至49.3%），而内需端进一步走弱（社零+0.4%、固投-8.1%、地产-21.6%），外需成为唯一有效对冲（出口+28.7%）。结构上，新动能（装
备制造+10.6%、高技术制造+17.6%、高技术产业投资+4.0%）仍是核心支撑，传统动能（采矿+0.7%、地产链、民间投资-11.4%）持续走弱。增
长动能正从地产/传统基建向新质生产力切换，但切换进度不足以熨平地产缺口。
逆周期政策：财政端，8000亿元新型政策性金融工具已于10月28日全部投放完毕，四季度加发地方政府债券，协同推进"十五五"规划与"六张网"重
大项目开工，推动存量与增量政策协同发力；货币端延续适度宽松，10月末社融与M2增速均高于名义GDP，流动性充裕，并已出台降低抵押补充贷
款利率、增加再贷款额度、居民住房贷款贴息等增量政策；扩内需方面，金融支持服务业扩能提质19条政策落地，聚焦服务消费与"两重""两新"衔

接；产业端深化"人工智能+"、加快新旧动能转换，稳定房地产市场并实施一揽子化债方案。发改委明确将及时出台务实管用的增量政策，努力实
现"十五五"良好开局。
风险提示：需持续关注的核心变量——①国内需求不足能否随逆周期政策显效而改善；②房地产链条能否止跌企稳；③外需不确定性；④财政货币
协同下流动性释放与实物工作量形成节奏。整体看，经济"稳中向好基础还需巩固"，后续政策发力节奏与经济内生动能修复将是关键。

### Page 14

数据来源：国家统计局、浙商期货投研数据

### Page 3

欧州
(株)日本総合研究所 欧州経済展望 2026年11月
◆７～９月期の成長率は前期から低下
足元のユーロ圏景気は緩やかに減速。７～９月
期の実質ＧＤＰ成長率は前期比年率＋1.7％と、
前期から伸びが鈍化。多国籍企業による事業活動
の影響で変動が激しいアイルランドを除いた成長率
は同＋１％割れと、潜在成長率を下回る水準。
国別の成長率をみると、スペインが前期比年率＋
2.6％と堅調な伸びであったものの、ドイツやイタリア
が減速したほか、フランスはマイナス成長に。
◆当面、フランスがユーロ圏景気の重石に
先行きも、スペインが下支えとなる一方、フランスが
重石となることで、ユーロ圏景気は減速が続く見通し。
物価高が個人消費を下押しするほか、以下２点が
フランスを中心に設備投資を抑制する公算。
第１に、不確実性の増大。10月中旬以降、フラ
ンスでは、財政再建策への反発や、物価高や教育
など公共サービスへの不満を背景に、各地で大規模
デモや暴動が発生。こうした動きは他の主要国にも
波及。経済・社会情勢を巡る不確実性の高まりが、

企業による設備投資の手控えにつながる可能性。
第２に、金融環境の引き締まり。ＥＣＢの利上げ
や財政・政治不安の強まりを背景に、各国の長期
金利が上昇。加えて、フランスの銀行は、地政学的
動向などを巡るリスク認識の高まりや、リスク許容度
の低下を理由に、企業向け融資基準を厳格化。
なお、足元にかけて高成長が続くスペインも、政策
面から景気の勢いが弱まるリスクあり（詳細はp.4）。
